Provenance: Variation #1001 for theorem in domain Geometry

New proofs (+50 from extended theorem bank)

Proof 1: Euler line: centroid, circumcenter, orthocenter are collinear

Statement: Euler line: centroid, circumcenter, orthocenter are collinear

Hypotheses: triangle ABC, G centroid, O circumcenter, H orthocenter

Conclusion: G, O, H are collinear with HG = 2*GO

Definitions: Euler line, centroid, circumcenter, orthocenter

Proof:

Step 1: Use vectors with origin at circumcenter O

Step 2: G = (A+B+C)/3

Step 3: H = A+B+C (Euler formula)

Step 4: H = 3G, so O,G,H collinear

Step 5: OG = |G-O|, OH = |H-O| = 3|G-O|

Step 6: HG = OH - OG = 2*OG

Proof 2: Nine-point circle: passes through 9 special points of triangle

Statement: Nine-point circle: passes through 9 special points of triangle

Hypotheses: triangle ABC

Conclusion: circle through midpoints, feet of altitudes, Euler points

Definitions: nine-point circle, Euler circle, Feuerbach

Proof:

Step 1: Let M_a, M_b, M_c be midpoints of sides

Step 2: Let H_a, H_b, H_c be feet of altitudes

Step 3: Let E_a, E_b, E_c be midpoints of AH, BH, CH

Step 4: Show all 9 points equidistant from center

Step 5: Center is midpoint of OH (Euler line)

Proof 3: Stewart's theorem: b^2*m + c^2*n = a(d^2 + mn) for cevian d

Statement: Stewart's theorem: b^2*m + c^2*n = a(d^2 + mn) for cevian d

Hypotheses: triangle ABC, D on BC, BD=m, DC=n, AD=d, AB=c, AC=b

Conclusion: b^2*m + c^2*n = a(d^2 + mn)

Definitions: Stewart, cevian, side lengths

Proof:

Step 1: Apply law of cosines to triangles ABD and ACD

Step 2: In ABD: c^2 = d^2 + m^2 - 2dm*cos(angle)

Step 3: In ACD: b^2 = d^2 + n^2 + 2dn*cos(angle)

Step 4: Multiply first by n, second by m, add

Step 5: Simplify using a = m+n

Proof 4: Ptolemy's theorem: for cyclic quadrilateral, AC*BD = AB*CD + BC*AD

Statement: Ptolemy's theorem: for cyclic quadrilateral, AC*BD = AB*CD + BC*AD

Hypotheses: ABCD cyclic (inscribed in circle), diagonals AC, BD

Conclusion: AC*BD = AB*CD + BC*AD

Definitions: Ptolemy, cyclic quadrilateral, diagonal

Proof:

Step 2: Show triangles ABE and DBC similar

Step 3: Show triangles ABD and EBC similar

Step 4: AB/BE = BD/DC => AB*DC = BD*BE

Step 5: AD/BE = BD/BC => AD*BC = BD*EC

Step 6: Add: AB*CD + AD*BC = BD*(BE+EC) = BD*AC

Proof 6: Law of cosines: c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab*cos(C)

Statement: Law of cosines: c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab*cos(C)

Hypotheses: triangle ABC, sides a,b,c, angle C opposite c

Conclusion: c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab*cos(C)

Definitions: law of cosines, side, angle

Proof:

Step 1: Place C at origin, B on x-axis

Step 2: A = (b*cos(C), b*sin(C))

Step 3: B = (a, 0)

Step 4: c^2 = |AB|^2 = (a-b*cos(C))^2 + (b*sin(C))^2

Step 5: = a^2 - 2ab*cos(C) + b^2*cos^2(C) + b^2*sin^2(C)

Step 6: = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab*cos(C)

Proof 9: Reuleaux triangle has constant width

Statement: Reuleaux triangle has constant width

Hypotheses: Reuleaux triangle constructed from equilateral triangle

Conclusion: width is constant in all directions

Definitions: Reuleaux, constant width, curve of constant width

Proof:

Step 1: At any angle, width = distance between two parallel supporting lines

Step 2: By symmetry of construction, width equals side length s

Step 3: Each vertex traces an arc of radius s

Step 4: Supporting lines always tangent to at least one arc

Step 5: Width = s for all directions

Proof 18: Gauss-Bonnet for surfaces: integral K dA = 2pi*chi(M)

Statement: Gauss-Bonnet for surfaces: integral K dA = 2pi*chi(M)

Hypotheses: M compact oriented surface without boundary

Conclusion: integral_M K dA = 2pi*chi(M)

Definitions: Gaussian curvature, Euler characteristic, compact surface

Proof:

Step 1: Triangulate M

Step 2: Compute angle excess at each vertex

Step 3: Sum of angle excesses = sum of (pi - angles) = integral K dA

Step 4: By Euler's formula: V-E+F = chi(M)

Step 5: Angle excess sum = 2pi*chi(M)

Proof 19: Hairy ball theorem: every continuous vector field on S^2 has a zero

Statement: Hairy ball theorem: every continuous vector field on S^2 has a zero

Hypotheses: S^2 unit sphere in R^3, v continuous tangent vector field

Conclusion: exists p on S^2 with v(p) = 0

Definitions: vector field, tangent, zero

Proof:

Step 1: Assume no zeros

Step 2: Define map S^2 -> S^2 by v/|v|

Step 3: This is a continuous map of degree 1

Step 4: But antipodal map has degree (-1)^3 = -1

Step 5: Continuous deformation preserves degree

Step 6: 1 != -1, contradiction
